I mean… outside of work, I wouldn’t want to say it’s terrible. I manage, mostly. It’s probably something I should be able to manage better, but it’s been getting in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar problems with staying organized or keeping track of tasks in other parts of your life, like at home or in personal projects?\n\nPatient: Mostly it shows up at work, or that’s where it’s causing the most trouble, I guess. The tickets and receipts and little workshop notes… customer jobs too… they all end up together, and then I look at the pile and I’m not sure what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nAt home, I mean, I try to keep things okay. It’s not like I’m trying to be careless. But the work part is what made me think I should come in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or keep track of things at home, even with personal responsibilities or chores?\n\nPatient: At home… I think it’s not as obvious, or maybe I just work around it more. The thing that really made me come in is work. I can’t seem to keep it in order anymore — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all get piled together, and then I’m standing there not knowing what I should do first.\n\nI know that sounds kind of basic, like I should just have a better system, but it’s been hard for me to keep track of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble organizing tasks or keeping track of things, even with everyday activities like grocery shopping, managing bills, or planning your day?\n\nPatient: I mean… outside of work, I wouldn’t want to say it’s terrible. I manage, mostly. But the thing that really feels like it’s gotten out of hand is at work — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs, all those little pieces of paper end up in one pile, and then I just sort of freeze because I don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nSo… I guess that is trouble keeping track of things, but work is where I notice it the most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it hard to get started on tasks, like chores around the house or even small projects, even if you know they need to be done?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I probably have times where I put things off like anyone does, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big problem at home.\n\nChores usually get done eventually. Maybe not always right away, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. The main thing I’ve been noticing is still more with work. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

But the work part is what made me think I should come in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or keep track of things at home, even with personal responsibilities or chores?\n\nPatient: At home… I think it’s not as obvious, or maybe I just work around it more. The thing that really made me come in is work. I can’t seem to keep it in order anymore — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all get piled together, and then I’m standing there not knowing what I should do first.\n\nI know that sounds kind of basic, like I should just have a better system, but it’s been hard for me to keep track of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble organizing tasks or keeping track of things, even with everyday activities like grocery shopping, managing bills, or planning your day?\n\nPatient: I mean… outside of work, I wouldn’t want to say it’s terrible. I manage, mostly. But the thing that really feels like it’s gotten out of hand is at work — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs, all those little pieces of paper end up in one pile, and then I just sort of freeze because I don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nSo… I guess that is trouble keeping track of things, but work is where I notice it the most. I mean… if something needs doing at home, I usually get to it at some point. Maybe I’ll put it off a little, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big issue the way the work stuff is.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m more on top of things than I am, but at home it just doesn’t stand out to me as much. It’s probably something I should be able to manage better, but it’s been getting in my way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed similar problems with staying organized or keeping track of tasks in other parts of your life, like at home or in personal projects?\n\nPatient: Mostly it shows up at work, or that’s where it’s causing the most trouble, I guess. The tickets and receipts and little workshop notes… customer jobs too… they all end up together, and then I look at the pile and I’m not sure what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nAt home, I mean, I try to keep things okay. It’s not like I’m trying to be careless. But the work part is what made me think I should come in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or keep track of things at home, even with personal responsibilities or chores?\n\nPatient: At home… I think it’s not as obvious, or maybe I just work around it more. The thing that really made me come in is work. I can’t seem to keep it in order anymore — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all get piled together, and then I’m standing there not knowing what I should do first.\n\nI know that sounds kind of basic, like I should just have a better system, but it’s been hard for me to keep track of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble organizing tasks or keeping track of things, even with everyday activities like grocery shopping, managing bills, or planning your day?\n\nPatient: I mean… outside of work, I wouldn’t want to say it’s terrible. I manage, mostly. But the thing that really feels like it’s gotten out of hand is at work — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs, all those little pieces of paper end up in one pile, and then I just sort of freeze because I don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nSo… I guess that is trouble keeping track of things, but work is where I notice it the most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it hard to get started on tasks, like chores around the house or even small projects, even if you know they need to be done?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I probably have times where I put things off like anyone does, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big problem at home.\n\nChores usually get done eventually. Maybe not always right away, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. The main thing I’ve been noticing is still more with work. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you're at home, do you ever find yourself avoiding starting tasks even when you know they need to be done, like cleaning or organizing something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I mean… if something needs doing at home, I usually get to it at some point. Maybe I’ll put it off a little, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big issue the way the work stuff is.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m more on top of things than I am, but at home it just doesn’t stand out to me as much. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: When you're at home, do you ever find yourself putting off starting tasks that you know need to be done, even if they’re small or straightforward?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not sure I’d say that happens much at home. I mean, I might leave something until later if I’m tired or if it’s not urgent, but I don’t really notice myself struggling to start small things the way I do with work stuff.\n\nI hope that makes sense—I’m trying not to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it hard to get started on small tasks, even if you know they’re simple or need to be done soon?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really that I’ve noticed. I mean, if it’s something simple and it really needs doing soon, I usually can make myself do it. Maybe I’ll drag my feet a little sometimes, but I don’t think it’s a major thing at home.\n\nI’m trying to answer it the right way—I just don’t want to make it sound like more than it is.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks—even small or simple ones—like picking up your clothes, doing a load of laundry, or making a phone call?\n\nPatient: I really don’t think so… not in a way that jumps out to me. I mean, I might not do laundry the second I think of it, or I’ll wait until I have more to wash, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck or can’t get started.\n\nPhone calls, maybe I’ll put them off if they’re not important, but I think most people do that, right? I don’t want to make it sound like I’m perfect at home, but I don’t know that it’s a big problem outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks—even small or simple ones—like picking up your clothes, doing a load of laundry, or making a phone call?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still say no, not really. I mean, I’m sure there are times I don’t jump right up and do something, like anyone, but I don’t feel like I’m fighting myself to start those little home things.\n\nIf I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “really hard,” you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s a big issue for me outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found it very hard to get started on tasks in general, even ones that aren’t at work—like schoolwork, hobbies, or even things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “very hard,” no. I mean, with things I enjoy, I usually can get myself going once I decide to do it. School was a long time ago, so it’s hard for me to judge that fairly now.\n\nI guess I can procrastinate like anybody, but I don’t really think of it as being unable to start in general. Unless I’m missing what you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found it very hard to get yourself to start tasks—even small ones—when you’re not at work, like getting ready for the day, starting a chore, or even doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, honestly. I mean, getting ready for the day—I just kind of do it because I have to. Chores I might delay a little, but I wouldn’t say it feels very hard to start.\n\nEven things I enjoy, if I want to do them, I usually can. I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for, but I haven’t really noticed that being a big problem for me outside of work.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work and there's no immediate pressure or requirement, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks — even ones you know you should do or even might enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. If there’s no pressure, I might put something off for a bit, but I don’t usually feel like I just can’t get myself started.\n\nI hope that makes sense. I’m not trying to give the “right” answer—I just haven’t really noticed that being a major thing for me outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, have you ever found it very hard to get yourself to start tasks, even ones that are important or that you want to do, like managing personal responsibilities or taking care of something that needs attention?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say “very hard,” no. I mean, if it’s something important, I usually do get to it—maybe not always right away, but I don’t feel stuck like I can’t start.\n\nI’m sorry if I’m not explaining it well. I guess outside of work, I just haven’t really noticed that being a big issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and even when there's no pressure, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks — even ones you know you should do or even might enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. I mean, if there’s no pressure I might be slower to get around to things, but I don’t usually feel like it’s this big struggle to start.\n\nMaybe I’m not thinking of the right kind of example, but outside of work I haven’t really noticed that as a major problem. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work and there's no one expecting you to do something, do you ever find it really hard to get started on tasks — even ones that you know you should do or that might help you feel better?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure I can say yes to that. If nobody’s waiting on me, I might take my time or kind of do it later, but I don’t usually feel like I can’t start at all.\n\nMaybe I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “really hard,” but outside of work, I haven’t noticed it as something that stands out. Does that sound like I’m answering it right?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If nobody’s waiting on me, I might take my time or kind of do it later, but I don’t usually feel like I can’t start at all.\n\nMaybe I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “really hard,” but outside of work, I haven’t noticed it as something that stands out. Does that sound like I’m answering it right?\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're not at work and there's no one expecting you to do something, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks — even ones that you know you should do or that might help you feel better?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / laundry_or_dry_cleaning_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in a way that feels really hard. If it’s just me and nobody’s waiting on it, I might putter around or leave it for later, but I usually don’t think of it as being unable to start.

The tickets and receipts and little workshop notes… customer jobs too… they all end up together, and then I look at the pile and I’m not sure what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nAt home, I mean, I try to keep things okay. It’s not like I’m trying to be careless. But the work part is what made me think I should come in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to organize tasks or keep track of things at home, even with personal responsibilities or chores?\n\nPatient: At home… I think it’s not as obvious, or maybe I just work around it more. The thing that really made me come in is work. I can’t seem to keep it in order anymore — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs… they all get piled together, and then I’m standing there not knowing what I should do first.\n\nI know that sounds kind of basic, like I should just have a better system, but it’s been hard for me to keep track of it. But the thing that really feels like it’s gotten out of hand is at work — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs, all those little pieces of paper end up in one pile, and then I just sort of freeze because I don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nSo… I guess that is trouble keeping track of things, but work is where I notice it the most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it hard to get started on tasks, like chores around the house or even small projects, even if you know they need to be done?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I probably have times where I put things off like anyone does, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big problem at home.\n\nChores usually get done eventually. Maybe not always right away, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. The main thing I’ve been noticing is still more with work. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you're at home, do you ever find yourself avoiding starting tasks even when you know they need to be done, like cleaning or organizing something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I mean… if something needs doing at home, I usually get to it at some point. Maybe I’ll put it off a little, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big issue the way the work stuff is.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m more on top of things than I am, but at home it just doesn’t stand out to me as much. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: When you're at home, do you ever find yourself putting off starting tasks that you know need to be done, even if they’re small or straightforward?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not sure I’d say that happens much at home. I mean, I might leave something until later if I’m tired or if it’s not urgent, but I don’t really notice myself struggling to start small things the way I do with work stuff.\n\nI hope that makes sense—I’m trying not to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it hard to get started on small tasks, even if you know they’re simple or need to be done soon?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really that I’ve noticed. I mean, if it’s something simple and it really needs doing soon, I usually can make myself do it. Maybe I’ll drag my feet a little sometimes, but I don’t think it’s a major thing at home.\n\nI’m trying to answer it the right way—I just don’t want to make it sound like more than it is.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks—even small or simple ones—like picking up your clothes, doing a load of laundry, or making a phone call?\n\nPatient: I really don’t think so… not in a way that jumps out to me. I mean, I might not do laundry the second I think of it, or I’ll wait until I have more to wash, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck or can’t get started.\n\nPhone calls, maybe I’ll put them off if they’re not important, but I think most people do that, right? I don’t want to make it sound like I’m perfect at home, but I don’t know that it’s a big problem outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks—even small or simple ones—like picking up your clothes, doing a load of laundry, or making a phone call?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still say no, not really. I mean, I’m sure there are times I don’t jump right up and do something, like anyone, but I don’t feel like I’m fighting myself to start those little home things.\n\nIf I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “really hard,” you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s a big issue for me outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found it very hard to get started on tasks in general, even ones that aren’t at work—like schoolwork, hobbies, or even things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “very hard,” no. I don’t know if that’s the kind of answer you’re looking for, but I haven’t really noticed that being a big problem for me outside of work.\n\nDoctor: When you're not at work and there's no immediate pressure or requirement, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks — even ones you know you should do or even might enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have trouble organizing tasks or keeping track of things, even with everyday activities like grocery shopping, managing bills, or planning your day?\n\nPatient: I mean… outside of work, I wouldn’t want to say it’s terrible. I manage, mostly. But the thing that really feels like it’s gotten out of hand is at work — the tickets, receipts, workshop notes, customer jobs, all those little pieces of paper end up in one pile, and then I just sort of freeze because I don’t know what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nSo… I guess that is trouble keeping track of things, but work is where I notice it the most. Is that answering what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it hard to get started on tasks, like chores around the house or even small projects, even if you know they need to be done?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I probably have times where I put things off like anyone does, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big problem at home.\n\nChores usually get done eventually. Maybe not always right away, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is. The main thing I’ve been noticing is still more with work. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: When you're at home, do you ever find yourself avoiding starting tasks even when you know they need to be done, like cleaning or organizing something?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it avoiding, exactly. I mean… if something needs doing at home, I usually get to it at some point. Maybe I’ll put it off a little, but I haven’t really noticed it being a big issue the way the work stuff is.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m more on top of things than I am, but at home it just doesn’t stand out to me as much. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: When you're at home, do you ever find yourself putting off starting tasks that you know need to be done, even if they’re small or straightforward?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not sure I’d say that happens much at home. I mean, I might leave something until later if I’m tired or if it’s not urgent, but I don’t really notice myself struggling to start small things the way I do with work stuff.\n\nI hope that makes sense—I’m trying not to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it hard to get started on small tasks, even if you know they’re simple or need to be done soon?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really that I’ve noticed. I mean, if it’s something simple and it really needs doing soon, I usually can make myself do it. Maybe I’ll drag my feet a little sometimes, but I don’t think it’s a major thing at home.\n\nI’m trying to answer it the right way—I just don’t want to make it sound like more than it is.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks—even small or simple ones—like picking up your clothes, doing a load of laundry, or making a phone call?\n\nPatient: I really don’t think so… not in a way that jumps out to me. I mean, I might not do laundry the second I think of it, or I’ll wait until I have more to wash, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck or can’t get started.\n\nPhone calls, maybe I’ll put them off if they’re not important, but I think most people do that, right? I don’t want to make it sound like I’m perfect at home, but I don’t know that it’s a big problem outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find it really hard to get yourself to start tasks—even small or simple ones—like picking up your clothes, doing a load of laundry, or making a phone call?\n\nPatient: I think I’d still say no, not really. I mean, I’m sure there are times I don’t jump right up and do something, like anyone, but I don’t feel like I’m fighting myself to start those little home things.\n\nIf I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “really hard,” you can tell me, but I don’t think that’s a big issue for me outside of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found it very hard to get started on tasks in general, even ones that aren’t at work—like schoolwork, hobbies, or even things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “very hard,” no.
